How RetractionWatch works

This module checks references and warns for citations in the RetractionWatch Database.

The RetractionWatch Check module compares the reference list against studies in the RetractionWatch database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that the study was retracted, has an expression of concern, or a correction.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the authors are already aware that a study was retracted, but the module can’t evaluate this.

The database can be manually updated with the rw_update function. For more information, see https://gitlab.com/crossref/retraction-watch-data.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Replication Check works

This module checks references and warns for citations of original studies for which replication or reproduction studies exist in the FLoRA database.

The Replication Check module compares the reference list against studies in the FLoRA (FORRT Library of Replication Attempts) database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that a replication or reproduction of the original study exists, and should be cited. No reminder is given when the paper already cites that replication or reproduction (matched by its DOI).

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the original study was cited for other reasons than the empirical claim tested, or that the replication/reproduction in the FLoRA database is for only one of the studies in the paper, and not the study the authors discuss.

The database can be manually updated with the FLoRA_update() function. For more information, see https://forrt.org/FLoRA/.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens, Lisa DeBruine and Lukas Wallrich

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Full text

Abstract

The data, analysis code, and appendices that support the findings of this study are available at https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.34469952.

1. Introduction

How do children learn words? The question has been central to developmental and language acquisition research for decades, and it has produced a durable theoretical tension about the nature of early linguistic knowledge. Some views hold that word learning is a continuous, incremental process in which children gradually refine their understanding through repeated exposure and cognitive development. Others emphasize moments of abrupt acquisition, in which a word is learned from very few exposures. Accumulator models sit between the two, pairing gradual accumulation of input with a discrete threshold at which a word counts as learned. In this paper, we raise the possibility that how word learning is measured contributes to this theoretical divide. Consistent with this, prior studies comparing binary parent report with continuous behavioral measures of the same word knowledge have yielded mixed findings (e.g., Fernald et al., 2006; Houston-Price et al., 2007; López Pérez et al., 2025; Smolík et al., 2023; Styles & Plunkett, 2009; Weaver & Saffran, 2026), suggesting that the word acquisition process may look differently depending on whether a binary or continuous measure is used. Thus, we consider two possibilities for the observed discrepancy: binary measures might miss intermediate stages of learning, or continuous measures might add noise to an essentially discrete process. To investigate these potential explanations, we conducted two studies. In Study 1, we adapted a common binary parent report measure of children's vocabulary knowledge into a continuous measure and examined whether parents' judgments of their children's lexical knowledge reflect graded understanding that a binary format may obscure. In Study 2, we assessed which parent report approach better predicted children's word recognition on a looking-while-listening task for the same words. Together, these studies ask whether the theoretical tension reflects something real about how children learn words, or whether it partly stems from how word learning is measured.

1.1 Theoretical Perspectives on Word Learning: Continuous, Discrete, and Accumulator

The continuous perspective of word learning emphasizes the gradual nature of linguistic development, where children's word knowledge is not an all-or-nothing state, but rather word representations strengthen with experience (Bloom, 2002; Meylan & Bergelson, 2022), supported by cognitive development (Samuelson, 2021). In this process, repeated exposure strengthens word-referent associations (Schneider et al., 2015; Weisleder & Fernald, 2013), with each encounter enriching the underlying representation: sharpening phonological detail, filling in semantic content, and building knowledge of the contexts in which the word is used (e.g., Dore et al., 2025; Goodman et al., 2008; Law & Edwards, 2015).

The discrete perspective holds that children can learn a word from a few exposures, using linguistic and contextual cues to build a novel word-object mapping. The result is apparent sudden knowledge of a word (Carey & Bartlett, 1978; Markson & Bloom, 1997). On propose-but-verify accounts, children generate an initial hypothesis about a word-referent association and, if correct, retain it after a single encounter (Trueswell et al., 2013).

Accumulator models occupy a middle position. These models propose that word learning involves continuous accumulation of input in a register through repeated exposure, and only once a critical threshold is reached is a word "learned" (Kachergis et al., 2022). While the accumulation process is continuous, they characterize learning as a discrete outcome (i.e., the endpoint of gradual accumulation).

1.2 Measurement Challenges and Inconsistencies

The divide between continuous and discrete perspectives of word learning is mirrored in, and may be reinforced by, the measurement approaches used to study it, particularly in how knowledge of individual words is scored. For example, continuous perspectives of word learning are typically supported by laboratory looking-while-listening tasks (Fernald et al., 1998), where children see paired images while hearing one of them named (e.g., "Look at the cookie!") and their eye gaze is recorded. These tasks yield graded outcomes for individual words: accuracy (i.e., proportion of looking time to target, ranging 0-1) and reaction time, both of which take continuous values even for a single word on a single trial (e.g., Sander-Montant et al., 2023; White & Morgan, 2008). Average performance on these measures improves steadily from six months (Bergelson & Swingley, 2012) throughout the preschool years (e.g., Fernald et al., 2006; Hurtado et al., 2014; Marchman et al., 2020; Peter et al., 2019; Sander-Montant et al., 2023). These studies therefore reveal gradual refinement in comprehension, which is the pattern a graded measure is likely to show.

Conversely, discrete and accumulator perspectives are typically supported by studies using binary measures, which score a word as "unknown" or "known," and therefore inherently conceptualize word learning as characterized by a discrete shift from not knowing a word to knowing it. One line of evidence supporting discrete views comes from fast-mapping studies, in which children given minimal exposure to a novel word-referent pairing have been found to have retained it (e.g., "bring me the chromium one, " Carey & Bartlett, 1978; "point at the blick," Spiegel & Halberda, 2011), although retention findings are mixed (Horst & Samuelson, 2008).

Accumulator models (Kachergis et al., 2022; McMurray, 2007) similarly rely on binary data to operationalize the threshold-crossing moment at which a word is learned. These models use production scores from parent reports like the MacArthur-Bates Communicative Development Inventories (CDI), in which parents indicate, for each word, whether their child says it. Other measures such as standardized language tests (e.g., PPVT; Dunn & Dunn, 1981), object naming tasks (Gershkoff-Stowe, 2001) and touchscreen tasks (Friend & Keplinger, 2003), may similarly reduce performance to a binary outcome (unknown/known).

Whether binary scoring adequately captures the degree to which a child knows a word-the depth and quality of word knowledge-has been questioned (Hadley & Dickinson, 2018). Aggregate scores from these measures are nonetheless robust predictors of later cognitive and language skills (e.g., Marchman & Fernald, 2008; Marchman et al., 2018), so the concern is not with the instruments overall, but with the item: whether scoring each word as unknown or known obscures gradations that continuous measures reveal.

Supporting this possibility, comparisons of binary CDI scores with direct assessments have produced mixed results. For example, in looking-while-listening studies, children have shown above-chance comprehension for words parents marked as both unknown and known on the CDI (Houston-Price et al., 2007), whereas other studies have found comprehension stronger for or limited to words parents reported as "known" (Smolík et al., 2023; Styles & Plunkett, 2009).

In touchscreen tasks, children performed better for words parents reported as produced on the CDI (Poulin-Dubois et al., 2013). However, in naturalistic language samples, children sometimes produce words parents had marked as not produced on vocabulary checklists (Tardif et al., 1999). Reporting is also inconsistent across and within raters. When multiple caregivers rated the same monolingual Dutch children's receptive and productive vocabulary on the CDI, no child's caregivers had perfect agreement, with greater discrepancies for children with larger vocabularies (De Houwer et al., 2005). Moreover, U.S. parents of typically developing children and children with autism who completed the CDI twice in the same session provided inconsistent responses approximately 10% of the time; discrepancies largely involved words reported as produced and then as not produced (Arunachalam et al., 2022; see also Lorang et al., 2023). These inconsistencies suggest that forced binary judgments miss some of the complexity of children's developing word knowledge, even when the target is observable behavior like word production.

1.3 The Current Study

This study examined what a binary response format captures and what it obscures in parents' reports of their children's word knowledge, and whether the missing information is related to children's behavior. We administered the same type of measure (parent report) in both binary and continuous formats, which differs from prior work that has confounded measure type and format. Parents of 8-31 month-old monolingual and bilingual children learning English, French, and/or Spanish completed the standard CDI and a novel continuous adaptation, which we call the Likert Approach to Vocabulary Assessment (LAVA), on which they rated their certainty about their child's knowledge of 30 CDI words using a 6-point scale. Throughout our discussions of this measure, we use continuous as shorthand for a graded, multi-point response format, in contrast to the CDI's binary format, and not as a claim about the underlying measurement scale, which is ordinal. Because CDI comprehension data come from the Words and Gestures form, comprehension analyses were restricted to 8-to 18-month-olds, while production analyses spanned the full age range.

LAVA measures parents' certainty rather than children's knowledge directly, but we argue that certainty offers a window onto knowledge if parents are attuned to their child's behaviors and experiences: high certainty may reflect robust knowledge (or lack thereof), while moderate certainty may reflect knowledge that is still emerging (Lorang et al., 2023). We therefore treat certainty as a proxy for knowledge, an assumption tested in Study 1 and again against behavioral data in Study 2.

Study 1 examined: (1) the correspondence between binary CDI and continuous LAVA scores, (2) the certainty thresholds parents set before endorsing words as known on the CDI, and (3) whether child-level factors known to predict word learning-age, language exposure, word-specific exposure, and vocabulary size-predicted parents' LAVA certainty ratings. Following previous work (Lorang et al., 2023), we predicted that each factor would increase certainty, because factors that support stronger word knowledge also give parents more evidence on which to base a confident judgment (Bloom, 2002; Byers-Heinlein et al., 2024; Goodman et al., 2008; Law & Edwards, 2015; Schneider et al., 2015). If these factors are predictive, it would suggest parents are sensitive to meaningful variation in their children's developing knowledge rather than simply expressing their own idiosyncratic uncertainty.

In Study 2, we investigated whether binary (CDI) or continuous (LAVA) parent reports better predicted children's performance on a looking-while-listening task. If the continuous parent ratings capture meaningful gradations that binary measures miss, we expect the continuous report to show the stronger relationship with behavioral performance.

Beyond report format, whether parent reports and direct measures converge may also depend on whether comprehension or production is assessed. Comprehension precedes production developmentally, observable as early as 6 months (Bergelson & Swingley, 2012; Fenson et al., 1994), but how knowledge of a given word in one domain relates to knowledge of that same word in the other remains unclear. If word production reflects a higher bar of knowledge than comprehension, and parent reports of production rest more on directly observable evidence, convergence may be greater for production. We therefore examined the relationship between the measures for comprehension and production where the data allowed.

2.

Study 1: Methods The Human Research Ethics Committee at Concordia University granted ethics approval for this study (certification #10000439), which was conducted in accordance with the Declaration of Helsinki. The current study analyzed data collected at the Concordia Infant Research Lab from 2022-2024 as part of a larger pre-registered study (https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.34441941)foot_0 . However, as the analyses of the current study were exploratory, they were not pre-registered.

2.1 Participants

Participants were recruited through a database of interested families living in Montreal, Canada, and through social media advertisements. Children were aged between 8-31 months (M = 20.60, SD = 6.62, range = 8.72-31.20). They were either monolingual or bilingual, learning one or two of the following languages: English, French, or Spanish. The study had a longitudinal component, where families could choose to participate at up to three time points, with a minimum interval of 3 months between visits. During each visit, parental consent was obtained, families were administered several questionnaires (see Measures section below), and children participated in an experimental task in one or both of their languages. Eleven participants were excluded based on the following criteria: premature birth (n = 1), low birth weight (n = 5), vision or hearing loss (n = 3), and bilingual children with exposure of 10% or higher to any additional languages (e.g., third or fourth language; n = 2). The final sample consisted of 81 children. Of these, 80 children contributed data at Visit 1, 29 at Visit 2, and 10 at Visit 3. Data were collected between 2022 and 2024.

In a demographics questionnaire completed at the first visit, parents reported their child's ethnicity as 39% European, 23% mixed ethnicities (e.g., European-South Asian, European-Latin/Central/South American), 14% Latin/Central/South American, 13% not applicable, unknown, or not disclosed, and 11% Other as reported by parents (e.g., Canadian). Additionally, families reported high levels of education (65% held a bachelor's degree or higher).

2.2 Measures

2.2.1 Parent-Reported Vocabulary Measure: CDI

Parents provided information about their child's receptive and/or productive vocabulary using the Web-CDI (deMayo et al., 2021), an online CDI which includes several hundred words typically acquired in early childhood (396 items on Words and Gestures, 680 on Words and Sentences in the English forms). Depending on whether the child was monolingual or bilingual, parents completed one or two forms in the child's language(s) at each visit, yielding a total of 194 language-specific administrations across all visits, which form the unit of analysis below. Forms were administered in American English (Fenson et al., 1993), Quebec French (Trudeau et al., 1999), and/or Mexican Spanish (Jackson-Maldonado et al., 2003), based on the language(s) the child was acquiring. For children aged 8-18 months (n = 37), parents filled out the Words and Gestures form (81 total administrations across visits and languages), which records, for each word, whether the child understands it (does not understand/understands) and whether they say it (not produced/produced). Summing the items provides an estimate of children's overall receptive and productive vocabulary. For children aged 19-31 months (n = 52, 113 total administrations across visits and languages), parents completed the Words and Sentences form, which captures information solely about the words children produce and thus provides an estimate of their productive vocabulary. Eight children contributed data on both forms as they aged across visits. Across all visits and languages, children's receptive vocabulary ranged from 0-404 (M = 97.85, SD = 101.90) and their productive vocabulary (across both CDI forms) ranged from 0-661 (M = 135.62, SD = 176.17).

2.2.2 Likert Approach to Vocabulary Assessment: LAVA

The Likert Approach to Vocabulary Assessment (LAVA) is a 6-point Likert scale we developed for this study, designed to capture parents' certainty about their child's knowledge of specific words, following approaches used by Hanania (2021) and Mills et al. (1997 Mills et al. ( , 2004)). Parents rated their certainty about their child's comprehension and production, giving each word a score from 1-6 in each domain. Only the endpoints of the scale were labeled. For comprehension, a score of 1 indicated "Very sure my child does not understand [e.g., book]," and 6 "Very sure my child understands [e.g., book]." For production, 1 indicated "Very sure my child does not say [e.g., book]," and 6 "Very sure my child says [e.g., book]." Parents rated their children's knowledge of 30 items drawn from the CDI; fourteen were the words tested in the looking-while-listening task (see Study 2), selected to be highly familiar to children and to have consistent labels across dialects. The remaining sixteen words were selected so that each was known by roughly half of children at a different point in the 8-to 31-month range, based on Wordbank norms (e.g., ~50% of children comprehend "diaper/couche" at 10 months and "flower/fleur" at 19 months; Frank et al., 2017). This spread item difficulty across the age range and therefore increased variability in scores.

Each item was then matched to its equivalent in English, French, and Spanish CDI adaptations, so that the three versions of the LAVA tested the same 30 concepts. Native speakers of each language verified these item labels and adapted the rating instructions and anchors.

Parents completed the LAVA at each visit, rating comprehension first and then production. Parents of bilingual children rated all 30 items in each of their child's languages. For comprehension ratings for children aged 8-18 months (for whom CDI comprehension data were available), LAVA scores spanned the full scale (M = 3.38, SD = 1.92). For production ratings across the full sample (8-31 months), LAVA scores again spanned the full scale (M = 3.02, SD = 2.22). The LAVA is available at https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.34441941.

2.2.3 Language Exposure Questionnaire using MAPLE

The Language Exposure Questionnaire (Bosch & Sebastián-Gallés, 2001), using the Multilingual Approach to Parent Language Estimates (MAPLE; https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?OTuvpS is a measure that estimates relative language exposure: the proportion of time a child hears each language, across all sources from birth to the test date. This estimate was obtained at each visit through a structured parental interview. Relative exposure was treated as a continuous predictor (0-100%) to encompass the full range of monolingual and bilingual experiences (e.g., Kremin & Byers-Heinlein, 2021; Rocha-Hidalgo & Barr, 2023). Across all visits amongst children with any exposure to a given language, participants' English language exposure ranged from 4-100% (M = 51.33, SD = 24.55), French language exposure ranged from 4-100% (M = 63.97, SD = 27.35), and Spanish language exposure ranged from 14-100% (M = 57.97, SD = 27.32). These means are conditional on a child having any exposure to the language and therefore do not sum to 100%.

2.2.4 Word Exposure Scale

The Word Exposure Scale, developed by Moore and colleagues (2022), is a 5-point Likert scale in which parents report how often they believe their child hears specific words, which has been found to be associated with word frequencies derived from home audio recordings (Dong et al., 2026a). Native speakers adapted the scale into French and Spanish versions. At each visit, parents indicated the frequency with which their child heard each of the 30 words listed on the LAVA. The scale ranged from 1 to 5, where1 = never,2 = less than once per week,3 = a few times per week,4 = once per day, and5 = several times per day. Children's word exposure spanned the full scale (M = 3.35, SD = 1.40).

2.3 Procedure

One to two weeks prior to each visit, parents were sent a link to complete the online version of the CDI in the child's language(s). If the CDI had not been completed beforehand, parents were asked to fill it out on the visit day. At the visit, parents completed a demographics questionnaire, the LAVA in the child's language(s), the corresponding Word Exposure Scale, and were administered the Language Exposure Questionnaire using MAPLE. Parents could consult with other caregivers when completing the questionnaires. After the questionnaires were completed, children participated in a looking-while-listening task in their language(s), described in Study 2. At the end of their visit, children received a diploma and a small gift (e.g., a t-shirt or book) for their participation. Families who attended more than one visit also received a report of their child's vocabulary growth at the end of the study.

3. Study 1: Results

First, we examined the relationship between CDI and LAVA for the same 30 words tested on both scales. We computed each administration's mean CDI and LAVA scores and conducted Spearman's rank-order correlations because Shapiro-Wilk tests indicated non-normal distributions (all ps < 0.05). Results showed strong positive relationships between the CDI and LAVA for both comprehension (r = 0.85, p < .001; n = 81 administrations from 37 children aged 8-18 months) and production (r = 0.91, p < .001; n = 194 administrations from 81 children aged 8-31 months). This suggested the measures are closely, although not perfectly, related, indexing overlapping but non-identical information.

3.1 LAVA and CDI Correspondence

Our initial research question was whether the CDI's two response options mask graded word knowledge that the LAVA's six options would reveal. To investigate this, we compared the CDI and LAVA scores parents reported for each of the 30 words. For example, for each word (e.g., "flower") at each visit, we paired the parent's binary CDI response (0 or 1) with the LAVA score they gave that same word (1 to 6). We conducted parallel analyses for comprehension and production.

3.1.1 Comprehension: CDI vs. LAVA

Overall, among the 2,159 LAVA comprehension scores obtained for younger children (8-18 months) for whom we had a corresponding CDI comprehension score, approximately half of responses (49%) were at the extremes of the LAVA scale (1 or 6) and the rest (51%) were distributed across the middle range (2 to 5; see Figure 1). Of the words parents marked as "understood" on the CDI, 55.6% received the highest LAVA certainty rating (6, "Very sure my child understands"). Of those marked "not understood," 39.8% received the lowest LAVA certainty rating (1, "Very sure my child does not understand"). This initial finding suggests graded uncertainty underlies parents' binary comprehension judgments. Distribution of LAVA Scores and Correspondence with CDI Reports Note. Distribution of LAVA scores (x-axis) and counts (y-axis) for comprehension (left panel) and production (right panel) are shown. Color indicates the corresponding CDI response for comprehension (does not understand/understands) and production (not produced/produced).

3.1.2 Production: CDI vs. LAVA

Among the 5,533 LAVA production scores obtained for children across all ages (8-31 months) for whom we had corresponding CDI production scores, 78.3% were at the extremes of the LAVA scale (scores 1 or 6), while 21.7% were distributed across the middle range (2 to 5). This revealed that parents used extreme response options significantly more often for production than comprehension (X²(1) = 636.75, p < .001). Of the words marked as "produced" on the CDI, 74.7% were scored with the highest LAVA certainty rating (6, "Very sure my child says"). Of those marked not produced, 73.8% were scored with the lowest LAVA certainty rating (1, "Very sure my child does not say"). Parents' LAVA production ratings were therefore closer to binary than their comprehension ratings, though the 21.7% of responses that fell mid-range suggest that graded uncertainty also underlies parents' binary production judgments, albeit less so than for comprehension.

3.2 Certainty Thresholds in CDI Reporting

We sought to identify the LAVA certainty threshold where parents would be equally likely to report a CDI score of 0 (does not understand/does not say) or 1 (understands/says). When we designed the LAVA, we expected this threshold to be at the midpoint of the scale (3.5). To discover whether parents were using the LAVA as expected, we conducted separate mixed-effects logistic regressions for comprehension and production. In each model, item-level LAVA scores predicted item-level CDI responses with random intercepts for participants and items.

Results showed that LAVA scores significantly predicted CDI binary responses for both comprehension and production (both p < .001; see fixed effect output in Appendix A), again showing that parents' continuous certainty ratings systematically map onto their binary judgments. Critically, the analyses also revealed that, for comprehension, parents were equally likely to report their child "understands" or "does not understand" a word at a LAVA score of 4.71, well above the midpoint of the scale (3.5; see Figure 2). Likewise, for production, the corresponding LAVA score was 4.72, exceeding the midpoint to the same degree. These results suggest that parents generally need to be quite certain before reporting that their child understands or produces a word on the CDI, setting similarly high certainty thresholds for both domains. Predicted CDI Probabilities Across LAVA Scores Note. Predicted probabilities from mixed-effects logistic regressions for both comprehension (left) and production (right). In each plot, the x-axis represents LAVA scores, and the y-axis represents the probability of a CDI score of 1 (understands/produces). The logistic curve illustrates the relationship between LAVA scores and CDI probabilities. A dashed red horizontal line at 0.50 indicates the point at which a report of "known" and "not known" on the CDI were equally likely. A red dot highlights the specific LAVA score at which this probability threshold was reached. A grey vertical line marks the midpoint of the LAVA scale (3.5). Shaded bands represent 95% confidence intervals.

3.3 Preliminary Validation

3.3.1 Child-Level Predictors of Parental Certainty

Next, as initial validation that parents' LAVA ratings meaningfully reflect children's knowledge rather than idiosyncratic use of the scale, we examined whether variance in parents' certainty ratings on the LAVA could be explained by child-level factors known to play a role in children's word learning (age, overall language exposure, word-specific exposure, and total vocabulary size). For the dependent variable, LAVA scores were re-coded to reflect parents' level of certainty (1 or6 = high certainty, coded 2; 2 or5 = moderate certainty, coded 1; 3 or4 = low certainty, coded 0). We then ran nested linear mixed-effects models separately for comprehension and production, progressively adding each child-level predictor as an interaction with CDI score to test whether effects differed based on whether parents judged a word as known or unknown (see model syntax and further analysis details in Appendix B). All models included random intercepts for participants and items.

Results revealed that each model's fixed effects explained approximately 20% of the variance in parental certainty ratings (comprehension: marginal R² = 0.21; production: marginal R² = 0.19; rising to 30-37% when individual variability was included), indicating systematic relationships between child-level factors and parental certainty for each domain. A consistent and significant pattern emerged for both domains (see Appendix B for fixed effects outputs and figure): parents' certainty for words they reported "known" increased as children's vocabulary size, word exposure, and language exposure increased (βs = 0.04 to 0.44). However, increases in these same child-level factors were related to reduced certainty for words parents reported "not known," suggesting that parents were less confident that these words were truly not known by their child. Age was a significant predictor only for production, with parents expressing reduced certainty for both response types (reported not produced/produced) for older children. Together, these results provide initial evidence that parents' certainty ratings on the LAVA are meaningfully related to their children's experiences and linguistic development.

3.3.2 Further Item-Level Assessment: Word Exposure

As further validation, given that word exposure emerged as the strongest predictor of parental certainty across both domains (comprehension: β = 0.44; production: β = 0.28, both p < .001), we re-examined this relationship using raw LAVA scores rather than recoded certainty, so that direction as well as strength of belief was retained. Linear mixed-effects models tested whether the frequency with which parents reported their child heard each word (ranging from 1-5) predicted parents' LAVA scores (raw scores ranging from 1-6) for that word. Each model had random intercepts for participants and items (see Appendix C for fixed effects outputs and figure). A one-unit increase in word exposure (e.g., from "once a day" to "several times a day") corresponded to a 0.74 increase in LAVA comprehension scores (8-18 months; β = 0.53, p < .001), while for the full production sample (8-31 months) it corresponded to a 0.61 increase in LAVA production scores (β = 0.38, p < .001). Word exposure explained 32% of the variance in comprehension scores and 15% of the variance in production scores. Parents' ratings were therefore more closely tied to word exposure for comprehension than for production.

4. Study 1: Discussion

Study 1 revealed that, for both domains, parents made frequent use of the middle of the LAVA scale when given the option, suggesting a continuum underlies binary CDI scores, though it appears more pronounced for comprehension (half of ratings fell mid-range) than for production (a fifth fell mid-range). This asymmetry is consistent with prior work (Hanania, 2021; Lorang et al., 2023; Weaver & Saffran, 2026) and may arise because production is more directly observable, while comprehension must be inferred from behavioral cues (e.g., looking, grabbing, pointing). Parents also required high certainty (LAVA score of 4.7) before marking words as known (either understands or says) suggesting conservative CDI thresholds, a point we return to in the General Discussion.

Child-level factors explained approximately 20% of the variance in certainty ratings, suggesting that LAVA scores track children's developing knowledge and experience, though most of the variance remains unexplained. Specifically, for words reported known, parents may gain certainty as children gain proficiency with a language or word. For words reported not known, however, growing competence may instead make it harder for parents to be sure a word is genuinely absent from the child's vocabulary. Indeed, prior work has shown that as children's vocabulary increases, there is less agreement between parents' word-level reports and direct language assessments (Łuniewska et al., 2024). Further, for production, parents were less certain for older children, which may similarly reflect difficulty in tracking what children know as they grow. Alternatively, this reduction in confidence could be due to more limited opportunities to observe children's language abilities as non-parental care (e.g., daycare) is more common for older than for younger children (Houston-Price et al., 2007), particularly in bilingual contexts. As one parent noted on the LAVA, "She speak[s] French at school not at home so I am not sure how much she knows" (see also Byers-Heinlein et al., 2020). Conversely, parents may be confident that a word is not known when the relevant factors are clearly limited, for instance when a child rarely hears the word in question. In support of this possibility, one parent noted on the LAVA, "Las palabras que no se utilisan [sic] con frecuencia, seguro que no las va a comprender" ["Words that are not used frequently will surely not be understood"].

Together, this pattern and the high certainty thresholds for reporting words as known suggest parents may be attuned to more than simple presence or absence of knowledge. They may be sensitive to the gradual strengthening of knowledge along a continuum, waiting until it appears established enough to confidently report it as known. We explore this possibility in Study 2.

5. Study 2: Methods

>Study 2: Methods

5.1 Rationale

In Study 2, we tested whether the graded knowledge implied by parents' mid-range certainty ratings is detectable in children's behavior by examining whether binary (CDI) or continuous (LAVA) parent report measures of word knowledge better predicted children's performance on the looking-while-listening task. This task is an established behavioral measure of word recognition that assesses children's real-time speech processing (Fernald et al., 2008). We tested children on a subset of 14 words that parents had scored on both parent report measures. If word knowledge exists on a continuum, and parents are sensitive to it, we expect the continuous LAVA measure to capture gradations in word knowledge that the binary CDI measure obscures, and therefore to better predict behavioral performance. Conversely, if word knowledge is acquired discretely, then we expect the binary CDI measure to predict behavioral performance just as well as, or better than, the continuous LAVA measure.

We also considered that direct comparison of these measures could be confounded by child-level factors that are associated with both parent-reported vocabulary and looking-while-listening performance, such as age (e.g., Fernald et al., 2006; López Pérez et al., 2025; Peter et al., 2019; Sander-Montant et al., 2023), total vocabulary size (e.g., Fernald et al., 2006 Fernald et al., , 2013;; Hurtado et al., 2014; López Pérez et al., 2025), language exposure (e.g., Hurtado et al., 2014; Pearson et al., 1997; Sander-Montant et al., 2023), and word-specific exposure (e.g., Dong et al., 2026b; Kartushina & Mayor, 2019; Potter & Lew-Williams, 2023). For brevity, we refer to these collectively as child-level factors, although word-specific exposure varies across items as well as children. Thus, to determine whether any relationships we observed were tied to word-specific knowledge rather than to children's general developmental and linguistic profile, we first assessed whether CDI and/or LAVA were associated with these factors and, if so, controlled for them in our main analyses.

5.2 Participants

Participants were drawn from the same sample as Study 1. Beyond the exclusions applied in Study 1, participants in Study 2 were further excluded if they did not have looking-while-listening data (n = 4), had excessive data loss (50% or more missing data during the analysis window; n = 3), or had not completed at least 25% of looking-while-listening trials in at least one of their languages (n = 3), leaving 71 of the 81 children from Study 1. For comprehension analyses, the final subsample consisted of 36 children aged 8-18 months (M = 14.20, SD = 2.99, range = 8.72-18.80). For production analyses, the final sample consisted of 71 children aged 8-31 months (M = 20.50, SD = 6.62, range = 8.72-31.20)

5.3 Parent Report Measures

Study 2 used the same parent report measures described in Study 1, collected at each visit. For these analyses, we calculated total vocabulary size excluding words tested on the looking-while-listening task, to avoid overlap between predictor and outcome (following López Pérez et al., 2025).

5.4 Behavioral Measure: Looking-While-Listening

5.4.1 Stimuli

Fourteen stimulus words were chosen for high familiarity, with all words reported as understood by more than 10% of 9-month-old infants acquiring North American English, Quebec French, or Mexican Spanish based on Wordbank norms (Frank et al., 2017). All words had consistent labels across different varieties of a given language.

The words were then organized into seven yoked pairs based on multiple criteria. Yoked pairs were chosen to differ visually and phonologically, to avoid thematic (e.g., hand-spoon) and semantic (e.g., hand-foot) relations, and to match grammatical gender in French and Spanish. They were also selected so that one word was at least twice as frequently heard in infants' typical input as its counterpart, as the youngest infants may use this potential extralinguistic cue (Steil et al., 2021).

Visual stimuli consisted of real object pictures depicting the 14 words. They were standardized using GIMP software (2019) to create uniform 500 × 500 pixel squares on a consistent grey background. All pictures had similar salience as judged by the research team and underwent luminance matching using the SHINE color toolbox (Dal Ben, 2021). Four central fixation stimuli (flower, heart, pinwheel, citrus slice) were used as attention-getters at the center of the screen prior to each trial. The yoked pairs appeared in designated left and right areas of the screen, with presentation sides counterbalanced across trials.

Auditory stimuli consisted of prerecorded sentences spoken in infant-directed speech by native speakers of each language (Canadian English, Quebec French, and Colombian Spanish). Speaker language proficiency was verified using the Language Experience and Proficiency Questionnaire (LEAP-Q; Marian et al., 2007). Each sentence began with "Look!" in the tested language, followed by one of four carrier frames or its translation ("Look at the", "Can you find the", "Do you see the", "Where is the"). Each word appeared equally often as target and distractor, and sentence frames were presented equally across testing orders.

Auditory stimuli were edited using Audacity software Version 2.4.2 (2020) so that they would be presented to participants at 70dB intensity. Silence was added prior to each sentence to standardize noun onset timing at 4500ms from trial onset across all languages.

5.4.2 Procedure

On each visit (up to three longitudinal visits per participant), monolingual children were tested in one language while bilingual children were tested in two languages. Prior to the visit, children were pseudo-randomly assigned to one of eight testing orders, with bilingual participants additionally counterbalanced for language order (half were tested in their dominant language first).

During the visit, children were seated on their parent's lap facing an EyeLink 1000 Plus eyetracker (SR Research Ltd., 2021). Parents wore noise-blocking headphones with music so they would be unaware of the auditory stimuli and were instructed to remain neutral throughout the experiment. The experiment began with a five-point calibration procedure, followed by 28 trials per language, presented in two blocks of 14 trials each, so that each of the 14 words served once as a target and once as a distractor. Each trial began with a gaze-contingent central fixation stimulus (e.g., colorful pinwheel, flower) paired with a non-linguistic sound to capture children's attention. Trials proceeded only once the child fixated on this stimulus. Subsequently, two images appeared simultaneously on the screen while children heard the target image labeled in infant-directed speech (e.g., "Look! Look at the cookie"). Children's eye gaze was recorded throughout each trial. Colorful animations were presented every seven trials to maintain engagement. The task lasted approximately 6 minutes per language.

5.4.3 Dependent Variable: Proportion Looking to Target

Two areas of interest (AOIs) were established covering the left and right sides of the display screen. The dependent variable was children's proportion of looking towards the target, operationalized as children's looking time to the target AOI divided by total looking time to either AOI, within an analysis window of 360ms to 3000ms after noun onset.

Trials were excluded for excessive data loss (50% or more missing data during the analysis window) and parental interference (e.g., labeling objects, pointing to the screen). In the comprehension subsample, 395 trials (22%) were excluded, leaving 1,412 trials. In the full production sample, 841 trials (20%) were excluded, leaving 3,289 trials.

6. Study 2: Results

>Study 2: Results

6.1 Overall Performance on Behavioral Task

On average, children looked at the target 55% of the time in the comprehension subsample (SD = 9%, range = 38-80%) and 63% of the time in the full production sample (SD = 12%, range = 38-91%). An intercept-only linear mixed-effects model with random intercepts for participants and items tested children's performance against chance level (0.5). Results showed children's performance was marginally above chance for the comprehension subsample (p = .056) and significantly above chance for the full production sample (p < .001).

6.2 Correlation Analysis

First, we examined correlations between child-level means of the parent report scores (CDI and LAVA), child-level factors (age, vocabulary size, language exposure, word exposure), and the behavioral outcome, proportion of target looking on the looking-while-listening task (see Correlation Matrix in Appendix D). Correlations were conducted separately for the comprehension and production domains using Spearman's rank-order correlations as Shapiro-Wilk tests showed non-normality, and were adjusted for multiple comparisons using the Bonferroni correction.

Both CDI and LAVA were moderately to strongly correlated with child-level factors for both comprehension (CDI r = 0.39-0.93; LAVA r = 0.57-0.83) and production (CDI r = 0.43-0.94; LAVA r = 0.53-0.91). Additionally, both parent report measures correlated moderately to strongly with proportion of target looking for both comprehension (CDI r = 0.47, LAVA r = 0.43) and production (CDI r = 0.70, LAVA r = 0.65). Similarly, proportion of target looking also correlated moderately to strongly with child-level factors: age (r = 0.47-0.59), total vocabulary size (r = 0.47-0.69), and word exposure (r = 0.37), while language exposure did not correlate with proportion of target looking in either domain.

These results raised the possibility of confounding between the parent report measures and the child-level factors as both relate to children's looking-while-listening performance. Thus, we next examined whether item-level parent report ratings (CDI and/or LAVA) independently predicted children's recognition of those specific words on the looking-while-listening task, and if so, examined whether these relationships still held when accounting for child-level factors.

6.3 Item-Level Parent Reports and Trial-Level Looking

We ran separate linear mixed-effects models for comprehension and production in which item-level CDI binary scores and item-level LAVA continuous scores independently predicted trial-level proportion of target looking, with random intercepts for participants and items. While word-level CDI scores could conceptually be treated as a factor with two levels (i.e., not produced/produced), in order to more directly compare standardized effect sizes with the LAVA, they were treated as numeric (i.e.,0 = not produced,1 = produced). Note that other estimates (e.g., unstandardized coefficients, standard errors, p-values) are unaffected by this decision.

Results showed that both CDI and LAVA significantly predicted performance in both domains (all p < .001; see Appendix E for fixed effects output). For comprehension, being reported to understand a word on the CDI was associated with an 8% increase in target looking, and each one-point increase in LAVA certainty was associated with a 2% increase in target looking (i.e., moving across the five intervals of the LAVA scale corresponds to 10%, comparable to the 8% CDI effect). For production, being reported to produce a word on the CDI was associated with a 13% increase in target looking, and each one-point increase in LAVA certainty was associated with a 2.6% increase in target looking (again yielding a comparable 13% effect across the full LAVA scale).

Across domains, both measures showed larger effects for production (CDI β = 0.21; LAVA β = 0.20) than for comprehension (CDI β = 0.13; LAVA β = 0.11). Within each domain, the two measures produced similar standardized effects, with the CDI slightly larger in both cases, although we did not test this difference formally. This pattern of comparable predictive power held when examining the domain of production in the same 8-18 month subsample for which we had CDI comprehension data (CDI β = 0.12; LAVA β = 0.12; both p < .001). These results showed that both binary and continuous parent report approaches significantly and similarly predicted children's looking-while-listening behavior (see Figure 3), which was contrary to our prediction that LAVA would outperform CDI. Predicted Independent Effects of CDI and LAVA Scores on Proportion of Target Looking Across Domains Note. Predicted independent effects of CDI and LAVA scores on proportion of target looking for comprehension (top) and production (bottom). Left panels display CDI scores on the X axis (0 = does not understand/produce,1 = understands/produces) while right panels display LAVA scores. The y-axis displays the predicted proportion of target looking. Shaded ribbons represent 95% confidence intervals.

6.4 Model Comparisons Controlling for Child-Level Factors

Next, we assessed whether the trial-level relationships observed between parent reports and looking behavior held when controlling for child-level factors. To do so, we compared five nested linear mixed-effects models for each domain. Predictors were progressively added to the models as follows: all child-level factors (age, total vocabulary size, language exposure, word exposure), then CDI scores, LAVA scores, then CDI and LAVA scores additively, and finally an interaction of CDI and LAVA. All models included random intercepts for participants and items. Model comparisons using likelihood-ratio chi-squared tests, Akaike Information Criterion (AIC; Akaike, 1974), and Bayesian Information Criterion (BIC; Schwarz, 1978) identified the best-fitting model for each domain.

6.4.1 Best-Fitting Model: Comprehension (8-18 months)

For the comprehension domain, the best-fitting model included only child-level factors: trial_prop_target ~ age_months + language_exposure + word_exposure + total_comprehension_vocab + (1|participants) + (1|items). Adding CDI binary comprehension scores (χ²(1) = 3.15, p = .076), LAVA continuous comprehension scores (χ²(1) = 0.35, p = .55), both parent report measures (χ²(2) = 3.16, p = .21), or their interaction (χ²(3) = 4.74, p = .19) to this model did not significantly improve model fit.

Rather, in this best-fitting model, word exposure was a marginally significant predictor (b = 0.01, β = 0.06, p = .051), with each one-unit increase on the word exposure scale (e.g., from "once a day" to "several times a day") associated with a 1% increase in target looking. Total comprehension vocabulary size was also marginally significant (b = 0.000262, β = 0.08, p = .061). Thus, for comprehension, neither the binary (CDI) nor continuous (LAVA) parent report added predictive value beyond what child-level factors explained (see fixed effects output and figure in Appendix F).

6.4.2 Best-Fitting Model: Production (8-31 months)

In contrast to comprehension, results for production were more complex. Initially, both parent reports independently improved fit beyond child-level factors (both p < .01). However, when testing each measure's unique contribution (adding one parent report to a model already containing the other and child-level factors), only CDI added unique information (χ²(1) = 10.34, p = .001 for CDI vs. χ²(1) = 1.28, p = .258 for LAVA). The best-fitting model nonetheless included a CDI × LAVA interaction (χ²(1) = 5.31, p = .021): trial_prop_target ~ age_months + language_exposure + word_exposure + total_production_vocab + CDI_production_score*LAVA_production_score + (1|participants) + (1|items). This interaction indicated that LAVA carried no unique information on average across words, but it did for a subset of them.

This model revealed that the utility of the continuous (LAVA) parent report depended on parents' CDI responses (interaction term, b = -0.02, p = .021; see fixed effects output in Appendix F). Specifically, LAVA was predictive for words parents reported on the CDI as not produced, but not for those already reported as produced (see Figure 4). Child-level factors were also predictive of children's performance: older children (0.3% increase per month, p = .049), those with greater word exposure (1% increase per scale point, p = .018), and those with larger productive vocabularies (0.01% increase per additional word, p = .009) exhibited higher looking-while-listening performance, while overall language exposure did not predict performance (p = .135).

These results suggest that together the parent reports captured word-specific information beyond what child-level factors explained, with the continuous parent report adding value for words reported not produced. However, this raised a further question: among words reported as not produced, which ones does a continuous parent report inform us about? That is, does LAVA's predictive strength vary across the whole pre-production period, or is it concentrated in the words that are about to transition to production?

Figure 4

Predicted Effect of LAVA Scores on Proportion of Target Looking by CDI Status from Best-fitting Model Note. Predicted interaction between LAVA scores and CDI on proportion of target looking from the model including parent report interaction and child-level factors. X-axis displays LAVA scores (1-6) and y-axis displays proportion of target looking. Lines are colored based on the CDI report: produced (dark green) and not produced (light green). Shaded bands represent 95% confidence intervals.

6.5 Exploring the Utility of Continuous Parent Report (LAVA)

We capitalized on the longitudinal nature of our dataset by comparing LAVA's predictive utility for different types of not yet produced words based on their subsequent trajectory. Because we had access to data from multiple visits from the same participants, we could examine how parent-reported CDI production status changed between two consecutive laboratory visits. This subsample consisted of 24 children who contributed word-level data to one or more of three production trajectories, though no single trajectory included all children: No Production 0→0 (reported not produced at both visits, n = 264 trials, 20 participants, M age = 17.60 months, SD = 6.25, range = 9.47-26.60), Transition 0→1 (reported as not produced and subsequently as "produced," n = 122 trials, 18 participants, M age = 20.50 months, SD = 4.71, range = 13.50-26.60), and Production 1→1 (reported as produced at both visits, n = 179 trials, 16 participants, M age = 21.50 months, SD = 4.56, range = 12.0-26.60). All of the 14 words tested were represented in all trajectories.

We compared two nested linear mixed-effects models predicting target looking, progressively adding predictors: first LAVA and all child-level factors, then a LAVA × production status interaction term. Each model included only random intercepts for items tested, as a random intercept for participants resulted in singular fit. Likelihood-ratio chi-squared tests revealed that the interaction model provided the better fit (χ²(4) = 17.75, p = .001), suggesting that LAVA's relationship with looking behavior varied by production status.

To interpret this interaction, we conducted separate linear mixed-effects models for each group, controlling for child-level factors and including random intercepts for participants and/or items (see Appendix G for fixed effects output). Results revealed that only the Transition 0→1 group showed a significant LAVA effect, in which higher parental certainty ratings that a child knew a word were associated with greater target looking (b = 0.06, β = 0.40, marginal R² = 0.14, p < .001; see Figure 5). Further, LAVA showed no significant effect for words that never transitioned (No Production 0→0; b = 0.02, β = 0.09, marginal R² = 0.02, p = .216) or were already produced (Production 1→1; b = 0.001, β = 0.009, marginal R² = 0.04, p = .917). Child-level factors were not significant in any of the three groups (p > .10), except for total productive vocabulary size in Production 1→1 (b = 0.0003, p = .009). These results indicate that continuous parent ratings capture gradations for words in transition to production; however, given that the Transition group is small (18 children, 122 trials), we treat this result as suggestive.

Figure 5

Predicted Effect of LAVA Scores on Proportion of Target Looking at Time 1 Across Three CDI Production Status Groups Note. Predicted effect of LAVA scores on proportion of target looking at Time 1 from the best-fitting model across three CDI production status groups: No Production 0→0 (reported not produced at both visits), Transition 0→1 (reported not produced at Time 1 and subsequently "produced" at Time 2), and Production 1→1 (reported "produced" at both visits). The x-axis displays LAVA scores and the y-axis displays proportion of target looking. Shaded bands represent 95% confidence intervals.

7. Study 2: Discussion

Study 2 examined whether binary (CDI) or continuous (LAVA) parent reports better predicted children's looking-while-listening performance. The pattern differed by domain. For comprehension, both measures predicted performance when entered alone, but neither improved model fit beyond child-level factors, which themselves showed only marginal or non-significant effects. Three explanations are possible: the predictors shared substantial variance; the behavioral measure was less reliable in this younger group, whose performance was only marginally above chance; or the subsample was simply too small to detect an effect.

For production, both binary CDI and continuous LAVA scores individually improved fit beyond child-level factors, counter to López Pérez et al. ( 2025), who found that CDI item-level reports did not predict looking once child-level factors were controlled. The best-fitting model, however, included their interaction. Longitudinal analysis revealed that the continuous LAVA parent report was predictive specifically for words that parents would report as produced at the following visit, capturing variation in looking behavior for these words that a binary measure collapses by design. This result contrasts with Weaver and Saffran (2026), who found that parental certainty did not moderate the word-level relationship between CDI scores and looking behavior, though it did predict whether the two agreed when looking was scored simply as above or below chance. The discrepancy may stem from our longitudinal design, which allowed us to identify words in transition. Moreover, in the present study the effect held beyond child-level factors, which themselves predicted children's looking performance, as in prior work (Fernald et al., 2006; Hurtado et al., 2014; López Pérez et al., 2025; Peter et al., 2019; Sander-Montant et al., 2023). Together, these findings suggest that a binary parent report predicts differences in children's looking between words reported produced or not. However, continuous parent ratings can capture additional gradations in children's emerging word knowledge that are detectable in behavioral measures. We expand on the implications of these findings in the General Discussion.

8. General Discussion

The present research investigated how much of the tension between continuous and discrete perspectives on word learning reflect developmental phenomena and how much is a product of measurement. Across two studies, we compared binary and continuous parent reports of the same words in the same children, and tested which better predicted children's looking-while-listening performance.

Overall, our findings suggest that what we observe about word learning depends on how we measure it. While binary and continuous measures performed comparably for words reported known, the continuous measure was uniquely informative for words in transition to production where children may demonstrate emerging knowledge behaviorally but have not yet crossed the threshold for being reported as knowing a word. For comprehension, neither measure added to what child-level factors already explained. The choice of measurement may therefore depend on which aspect of word knowledge is of interest, but using both measures may provide a better picture than either alone. Rather than asking whether word learning is continuous or discrete, we might instead consider how both perspectives capture different but real aspects of development.

8.1 Theoretical Implications: Continuous Process, Discrete Thresholds

Of the perspectives considered, our empirical results align most closely with accumulator models, while pointing to a missing piece of this framework. These models propose two phases of word learning, whereby children continuously accumulate input in word-specific registers until crossing a threshold that marks "learning" (Kachergis et al., 2022; McMurray, 2007), without accounting for a period in which words are not yet learnable (i.e., no word knowledge). In line with this account, our continuous measure was sensitive enough to capture variation in children's word recognition during transition periods-consistent with knowledge accumulation. It likewise showed no variation for stably produced words, consistent with post-threshold "learned" words. Critically, however, it also captured no detectable variation for words stably not produced.

The absence of an effect for the No Production 0→0 trajectory could reflect insufficient power to detect small LAVA effects, as the trend is positive yet non-significant. However, this trajectory contained over twice as many observations as the significant Transition 0→1 trajectory. We therefore consider an alternative possibility: an additional developmental phase before accumulation even begins, where no meaningful word-meaning link has begun to build for that word. A mixture of words with no word knowledge and words in genuine early accumulation could also explain the positive trend. Thus, our empirical results raise the possibility of three distinct phases of word learning. Building on Kachergis et al. (2022), we propose a three-phase model of word learning consisting of pre-accumulation, active accumulation, and post-threshold knowledge (see Figure 6). Our data support this model for production. We expect a similar process for comprehension, supported by the same accumulation of experience, with a threshold typically crossed earlier than that for production. Throughout, binary scores may not overlap cleanly onto a single phase, as elaborated below.

Figure 6

Three-Phase Model of Word Learning Note. Conceptual illustration of the Three-Phase Model: a pre-accumulation phase where exposure instances (illustrated as droplets) do not yet contribute to word-meaning associations (no word knowledge), a subsequent active accumulation phase where exposure instances contribute to building word-meaning associations (partial word knowledge begins being accumulated as register fills), and a post-threshold phase, where the word is "learned." The lower panel shows a conceptual example of how the CDI and LAVA scales may map onto each phase. The figure is drawn for production. We expect the same phases for comprehension, with its threshold crossed earlier.

In the pre-accumulation phase, even repeated exposure does not yet build word-meaning associations, because not all words may be learnable at every point in development. For example, a newborn who hears "table" many times will not come to understand the word, presumably because learning it requires cognitive and/or linguistic prerequisites the child does not have (Bergelson, 2020). This is consistent with maturational accounts of word learning (Bergelson, 2020), and with computational work treating a word's learnability as a developmental variable (Tsui et al., 2022). Internationally adopted preschoolers beginning to learn English acquire some high-frequency words, such as "yesterday," earlier than infants at a comparable stage of exposure, which points to cognitive maturity rather than input as the limiting factor (Snedeker et al., 2012). Lexical exposure during this phase may still build precursors of word learning, including a language's sound patterns (Saffran & Thiessen, 2003), word segmentation (e.g., Saffran et al., 1996), and word form encoding (e.g., Swingley, 2007), without yet linking form to meaning. In the Three-Phase Model, words in this pre-accumulation phase are truly unknown to the child and would be reported as not known on the CDI, with high certainty on the LAVA (e.g., a rating of 1 or 2). For production, the phase contains words a child neither comprehends nor produces. A word that is comprehended but not produced has already crossed the comprehension threshold and belongs in the active accumulation phase.

Subsequently, in the active accumulation phase, experience with a word can now contribute to word learning, and here continuous measures may capture what binary measures miss. The 21.7% of production ratings that fell in the middle range of the LAVA scale in Study 1 may capture this transitional window, where children may have accumulated enough partial knowledge to be detected by looking-while-listening but have not yet crossed the threshold to established knowledge. For production (8-31 months), this accumulation window might be narrow and temporally precise. In turn, for comprehension (8-18 months), our continuous measure may have captured more of this developmental phase, given that 51% of parents' ratings fell mid-range. However, as our comprehension sample was restricted to 8-to 18-month-olds, this domain difference is confounded with age. Nonetheless, it may be that comprehension inherently involves more protracted, graded learning (Gershkoff-Stowe & Hahn, 2013; Hendrickson et al., 2015), or that younger children navigate an extended accumulation phase across most of their vocabulary (Bergelson & Swingley, 2012; Fernald et al., 2006), requiring many exposures to build representations. Moreover, if comprehension serves as a prerequisite for production, reported production may reflect that a child already has high comprehension of that word and perhaps a stronger, more stable representation of it, which could explain a narrower window for production. Future work should assess these possibilities. Ultimately, in this active accumulation phase, words might be reported as either known or not known on the CDI, with less parental certainty on the LAVA, reflecting their emerging rather than absent or established knowledge.

This pattern, with absence or consolidated knowledge at the endpoints and a learning period between them, aligns with prior work (Dore et al., 2025; Hendrickson et al., 2015 Hendrickson et al., , 2017)). Using children's eye gaze and touch responses in a forced-choice task, Hendrickson and colleagues (2015, 2017) identified three knowledge states: absence, partial knowledge, and established knowledge. Partial knowledge was captured by the continuous measure but missed by the binary measure. Dore and colleagues (2025) found a comparable progression in children's errors on a receptive vocabulary task, revealing incremental semantic gains that traditional binary assessments would miss. In both lines of work, children who showed partial knowledge were more likely to identify the word correctly later on than those who showed none, suggesting that partial knowledge supports the established knowledge that follows.

Lastly, in the post-threshold phase, word knowledge consolidates, and words would typically be reported as known on the CDI (CDI = 1), with high certainty. Children may still become faster and more accurate in their word recognition after a word is reported "learned" (e.g., Fernald et al., 2006). Nonetheless, for these stably produced words, binary measures were as informative as continuous ones.

The proposed three-phase model, consistent with prior findings, could help reconcile the continuous versus discrete debate. Studies demonstrating graded knowledge (Dore et al., 2025; Hendrickson et al., 2015 Hendrickson et al., , 2017) ) may be capturing children during active accumulation, while fast-mapping studies (Carey & Bartlett, 1978) may reflect rapid threshold-crossing for developmentally ready words. Critically, measurement approaches may determine which phase is visible: binary measures may mark post-threshold states but collapse gradations within the accumulation phase by treating absence of knowledge and emerging partial knowledge equivalently, thus losing information about intermediate knowledge states which may pave the way for later established knowledge. The apparent theoretical tension may thus partly reflect which developmental phase researchers study and which measurement tools they employ to observe it.

8.2 Understanding Parental Certainty: Genuine Partial Knowledge or Measurement Uncertainty?

We have argued that parents' mid-range LAVA ratings capture children's partial knowledge during the active accumulation phase. However, an important (but not mutually exclusive) alternative explanation must be considered: perhaps mid-range ratings also reflect parental uncertainty arising from challenges parents face in assessing children's word knowledge. If so, the apparent accumulation phase we observe might, at least in part, reflect assessment difficulty rather than solely a real developmental period, a possibility for both domains but particularly relevant for comprehension given the increased mid-range LAVA variability observed in Study 1. Although the current study cannot fully disentangle these possibilities, below we consider multiple potential sources of parental uncertainty that may contribute.

One contributor to parental uncertainty could be the inherent difficulty of assessing word knowledge, particularly for comprehension. To assess comprehension, parents must interpret nonverbal cues such as looking, pointing, and grabbing, which may not always be clear or consistent indicators of word knowledge in young children. For example, in highly controlled laboratory paradigms, children may show accurate looking but incorrect pointing (Hendrickson et al., 2015 (Hendrickson et al., , 2017; although see Creel, 2025). Moreover, early comprehension appears to be subtle and fragile, with children demonstrating knowledge differentially depending on the relation of one object with another (e.g., semantically related vs. semantically unrelated words; Bergelson & Aslin, 2017a , 2017b; Kartushina & Mayor, 2019). As one mother filling out the CDI expressed in a prior study: "I am not sure if word X is understood. I have ticked the ones most likely!" (Houston-Price et al., 2007). This observability issue could contribute to why 51% of comprehension ratings fell mid-range: in some cases, parents may genuinely not know whether their children know these words, regardless of the child's actual knowledge state.

Parents face fewer observability challenges for production, since articulated speech provides more direct evidence. Parents reported greater certainty in this domain, although 21.7% of ratings still fell mid-range, which could reflect challenges like distinguishing babbling from meaningful words (Vihman & McCune, 1994), interpreting over-and under-extensions (e.g., Rescorla, 1980), judging imitations versus true productions (Tomasello & Mervis, 1994), handling usage that varies across contexts (e.g., Bloom, 1973), and recalling which words children do and don't say once vocabulary grows large.

We cannot fully separate genuine partial knowledge from measurement uncertainty, but our central claim does not depend on doing so. Even if mid-range LAVA ratings reflect some combination of the two, binary measures collapse that variability by design, obscuring whatever mix lies in the middle range. Continuous ratings relate systematically to developmental factors and predict looking behavior during transitional windows, which indicates gradations in word knowledge regardless of their source. The binary question "does your child know this word?" thus oversimplifies what parents observe as language emerges.

8.3 Consequences of Conservative Thresholds

Parents in our sample required high certainty thresholds before marking words as known on the CDI: 4.7 out of 6 for both domains. These conservative thresholds may reflect a desire to report accurately, but they may also inadvertently underestimate vocabulary by overlooking partial or emerging knowledge. This could explain discrepancies between parent reports and direct assessments (Houston-Price et al., 2007; though see Styles & Plunkett, 2009 for mixed findings).

Thresholds may vary across populations and individuals. Reported vocabulary differs between British and U.S. samples and across socioeconomic groups in ways that may reflect reporting style in addition to or rather than children's knowledge (Carmiol et al., 2024; Feldman et al., 2000; Hamilton et al., 2000; Houston-Price et al., 2007; although see Ertaş et al., 2026), and individual parents may set thresholds idiosyncratically (Day et al., 2025). Where thresholds differ, continuous measures like LAVA may yield more comparable assessments by recording the full range of certainty rather than forcing classification at a threshold that shifts across reporters.

8.4 Implications, Limitations, and Future Directions

It is important to contextualize our findings within the CDI's original design goals. The CDI's binary format was intentionally created for efficient assessment of vocabulary breadth across hundreds of words, a goal it achieves well (Fenson et al., 1993). Its efficiency, reliability, and extensive norming data have made it an essential tool for research and clinical practice worldwide. Our results do not challenge the CDI's validity or utility for its intended purpose. However, our results do reveal that continuous measures like LAVA can capture emerging knowledge that binary formats cannot, which could aid in stimuli selection for experimental designs, depending on the research question.

In addition, our findings have clinical and research applications. In clinical settings, binary and continuous parent reports may work complementarily. While total vocabulary scores on parental reports could serve as a useful screening tool (Łuniewska et al., 2024), item-level scores could provide more nuanced information about words parents deem not known by their children on a binary measure, helping clinicians better distinguish words with emerging knowledge and provide more sensitive progress monitoring. For example, a child showing gradually increasing mid-range LAVA scores over time might still be making gains in knowledge, even if the word has been reported as not known in binary terms because it has not yet crossed the learning threshold. Targeting these emerging words may help foster established knowledge, while words marked as known with high parental certainty may indicate that the word is likely established in the child's lexicon. Moreover, if children with language delays tend to spend extended time in the accumulation phase before consolidation, this could signal that longer vocabulary interventions may be needed for successful acquisition (Dore et al., 2025). Crucially, future work is needed to validate continuous measures for clinical populations and determine optimal cut-offs for identifying delay.

Nonetheless, several limitations suggest caution in generalizing our findings. First, our sample was drawn from a population of French, English, and Spanish-speaking families in Canada, limiting generalizability, as both linguistic and cultural factors could influence the thresholds parents set for marking words as known.

Second, our behavioral task assessed children's word recognition for highly familiar nouns. Whether continuous parent reports provide similar utility for other word classes (e.g., verbs, adjectives, function words) or for words varying in properties (e.g., imageability, concreteness) remains unknown and should be assessed in future work.

Third, while our Study 1 models explained variance in parents' certainty ratings, substantial unexplained variance remains. Additional factors that may shape parental certainty, such as word-specific properties (phonological complexity, semantic category), whether parents share the target language with the child (particularly relevant for bilingual families), and whether exposure source (e.g., input received primarily through daycare) limits observability and therefore certainty, could be further examined.

Fourth, while our study included a behavioral measure of word comprehension, we did not have any behavioral measures of word production. Future work could examine how continuous LAVA certainty ratings relate to the characteristics of children's actual home productions through language environment recordings (e.g., LENA) or speech elicitation. This could reveal which features of emerging productions-phonological accuracy, consistency, frequency, contextual appropriateness-parents track when rating certainty, and how LAVA ratings shift as productions become more mature, reliable, and established.

Relatedly, future work should further assess whether comprehension and production are distinct, unitary, or interwoven domains (e.g., Benedict, 1979; Chater et al., 2016; Pickering & Garrod, 2013), particularly given their moderate-to-high correlation in prior work (Feldman et al., 2000; Marchman et al., 2018) and the current study (r = 0.75). Because our comprehension analyses included only younger children (8-18 months), the domain differences we reported are confounded with age, and future research should examine if differences arise across domains in the same older children.

9. Conclusion

This research demonstrates that measurement approaches shape-but do not fully determine-what we observe about early word knowledge. Rather than concluding whether word learning is continuous or discrete, we propose a three-phase model: a pre-accumulation period where words are not yet learnable, an active accumulation period where partial knowledge builds gradually, and a post-threshold period where knowledge consolidates. By recognizing that word learning involves distinct developmental periods requiring different measurement approaches, we can develop richer theoretical accounts of how children transform linguistic input into communicative competence.
